#7394. Software Engineer - AI Tooling Infrastructure at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, AI Tooling Infrastructure to join our AI Tooling team and help build and run the backend services behind Supabase’s AI products. The Supabase MCP server is one example of what the team runs in production, alongside the services that support agent skills and the dashboard assistant. You’ll work closely with the Platform, SRE, Auth, Billing and Edge Functions teams, and with the engineers on the AI Tooling team who build the product surfaces.

The AI Tooling team runs its own services in production and carries its own on-call. Traffic from AI clients and agents behaves differently from traffic from people: hundreds of calls a minute, retry loops, and no reading of deprecation warnings. This role makes sure the services they use are reliable, correctly authorized and observable. It is a systems and infrastructure role rather than a product AI role, and it is not an SRE role. You will build and operate these systems, not only advise on them.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments, owns what they ship in production, and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and operate the production backend services behind Supabase’s AI products, including the MCP server: deployment, scaling, configuration and release.

Take ownership of how those services are structured, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Define, measure and meet reliability targets for the services the team runs. Set SLOs with the SRE team, track error budgets, and use them to decide what to fix first.

Participate in the AI Tooling team on-call rotation, and be a first responder for incidents in those services. Investigate, fix, coordinate with other teams during incidents, and write the follow-ups and runbooks.

Work the AI Tooling team support queue alongside on-call, and turn recurring issues into fixes rather than repeated manual answers.

Own authentication and authorization for AI and agent clients: OAuth apps, scoped tokens, and permissions that make sense for agents as well as humans, including how we change or deprecate a permission without breaking existing integrations.

Own observability for the services the team runs: metrics, traces, logs, dashboards and alerts, so we find problems before customers do.

Handle capacity and abuse for agent traffic: rate limits, cost and safety controls, and behavior under load or when a dependency degrades.

Improve the architecture of the services the team owns as they grow, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Work with the Platform team on shared code, review ownership and release process, and with the SRE team on infrastructure, alerting and incident practice.

Contribute to the code of the AI Tooling team’s tools, such as the MCP server, where it affects reliability and security, in partnership with the engineers who own the product surface.

Have 5+ years of experience building and running backend services in production, and have carried on-call for them.

Write strong TypeScript or Go, and are comfortable working in the other.

Have designed and operated APIs with real consumers: versioning, error handling, rate limiting, and backwards-compatible change.

Know OAuth, OIDC, JWT and API authorization well enough to debug them in production and to explain where implementations commonly deviate from the spec.

Have a good understanding of PostgreSQL and relational databases.

Are comfortable with CI/CD, Docker and containerized deployments, and with infrastructure as code. Any tool is fine.

Can debug across services: follow a slow or failing request through several systems using metrics, traces and logs.

Communicate clearly in writing, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly.

Pick your centre of gravity. We expect you to be deep in at least one of these, and to tell us which:

APIs and auth: You have designed permission models, token scopes or OAuth flows for third-party or machine clients, and you have migrated them without breaking users.

Operations: You have run production services yourself: carried the pager, written alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix, and made shared systems less risky over time.

Gateways and distributed systems: You have built or run proxies or gateways, or debugged hard failures across services (retries, partial failures, upstream outages, fail-open vs fail-closed decisions), and you can reason about load and degradation.

Experience with agent tooling (MCP or similar), or serving traffic from AI agents and LLM clients.

Experience moving a service out of a larger codebase while keeping existing clients working.

Experience with Grafana, OpenTelemetry, Logflare or a similar observability stack, and real SLO work.

Open-source contributions, especially to Node.js, Go, TypeScript or API tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7393. Twitch debuts shoppable, skippable ads as TwitchCon feels growing pains

A pair of new ad products unveiled around the gathering are meant to shore up the livestreaming service’s ability to meet full-funnel marketing needs.

New ad launches for Twitch are part of Amazon’s efforts to turn the livestreaming service into a full-funnel advertising channel while shoring up its image as a place that consistently attracts a young, highly engaged user base. Amazon is trying to position Twitch as a destination for sustained consumer attention but TwitchCon, its signature showcase, has been wrestling with growing pains related to creators of late.

Twitch viewers spend over four hours a day on the site, on average, and many creators are known for hosting hourslong streams that feature frequent interactions with their chats. Nearly 70% of Twitch’s global audience is Gen Z and millennials, many of whom can’t be found on Prime Video, the announcement said. Ninety-two percent of ads on Twitch are seen by viewers, above averages for other social platforms, per new research that measurement firm Lumen conducted on behalf of Amazon.

Shoppable ads are one way for Amazon to demonstrate that Twitch campaigns can be tied directly to business results. The format appears as an overlay to Twitch video ads and works in a similar fashion to interactive video ads on Prime Video. Users can add products featured in the Twitch ad to their Amazon cart, browse different styles and buy directly from the e-commerce site, shortening the path from brand message to transaction. Twitch shoppable ads can be bought alongside Prime Video inventory via the Amazon Ads Agent.

Any shoppable products will already be on Amazon, meaning brands do not need to create a separate landing page or individual creative. Twitch will bring the format to Europe, the Middle East and North Africa and Asia Pacific throughout H1 next year, and later plans to introduce iterations designed for Twitch on mobile and connected TV.

Skippable ads appear to be a way for Amazon to court a wider range of brands to Twitch, since the option is promoted as being more budget-friendly. Skippable ads are self-service and performance-oriented, with advertisers only paying when a viewer remains engaged. Razer, the gaming hardware maker, saw a 2.44 times higher return on ad spend when testing the format in the U.S., about double the ROAS of prior Twitch campaigns focused on standard video. One-third of the customers reached with skippable ads campaigns were new to the brand.

TwitchCon’s status as a must-attend destination for top streamers and sponsors has been challenged due to concerns around personal safety and changing creator needs, Digiday reported earlier this week. Creator marketing has evolved from a secondary consideration for many marketers into a core media channel, and the presence of creators at other industry conferences, such as Cannes Lions and Advertising Week, has intensified.

Amazon does not break out Twitch’s revenue in earnings reports, but the e-commerce giant’s overall rate of advertising growth has slowed. Revenue derived from ad sales was up 12% year over year to $19.6 billion in Q3 2026 compared to 22% during the same period last year.

The sense of a stable middle ground is eroding across an industry at a point of contraction, with many displaying mixed feelings about marketing’s future.

#7389. Mars wants to future proof impulse buys as AI is poised to take over more grocery runs

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

When AI does the grocery shopping, what happens to the impulse snack purchase at checkout? That’s the question Mars is grappling with.

The conglomerate, which owns snack brands like Dove chocolate, M&Ms and Pop-Tarts, is hedging its bets against an agentic future. Neil Reynolds, global chief customer officer at Mars, doesn’t expect AI to kill the in-store checkout lane, but he expects that agentic shopping assistants will take over more of the weekly grocery run. At least that’s the case based on Mars’ recent future of shopping and retail report penned in partnership with Kantar and Synthesis.

Digiday caught up with Reynolds to discuss how Mars is trying to sell products to AI, the upshot of AI visibility and the future of impulse purchases in an agentic landscape.

This interview has been lightly edited for clarity.

For us, the road map sits against those two parallel parts… how can we build the right capabilities internally to make sure our categories and our brand really shine in agentic commerce. That means, how do we make sure our brands are discoverable by the LLMs and how do we make sure that once those brands are discovered, that they convert through to purchase? The second milestone plan, or the second road map, is around, how do we start to step into building these experiential, immersive retail destinations for our stores? How do we partner with our retailers to think about, in effect, creating different cultural environments, and building a different experience when you step into a store?

The first thing we’re trying to do is sort of get some real data around what’s the start point for how our categories and how our brands show up. What that looks like for us is trying to get data through partnerships with emerging tech companies to get a start point for how we can show up in the LLM. What’s the data that, in effect, is showing up and where’s that data being sourced from?

Part of this genuinely is about connectivity between the teams. We’re setting up what we call One Demand performance squads — which are, again, how do you get the brand manager next to the insights person next to the key account manager next to the category manager, and the agency partner?

Winning this consumer journey through effortless, personalized and experiential, will be within the Publicis functionality or contract as well now. Our historical relationships are more been around media and creative, and what we’ve now got with Publicis is an end-to-end agency partner who can also support us with commerce. We can unlock some of the data that they have. They talk about connected ID being part of their offer, and then we can ideate to build those effortless, personalized experiential shopper experiences.

Back in the day, we would have a salesperson from Mars going to see a commercial buyer from a retailer. But we’ve quite quickly worked out that if we want to be strategic partners, we need to bring more people to that conversation. For example, how can we have a tech-to-tech conversation. Why is our data team not talking to the data team at Walmart?

We have ongoing conversations with many of the key leading retailers around, call it “store of the future initiatives” — and how we can ideate around continuing to create even more immersive and engaging shopping experiences for snacking in their stores.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

Mattel’s TikTok Shop store debuts this week with roughly 20 products across Barbie, Hot Wheels, Fisher-Price and Monster High properties.

The case for doing less with creators, according to Guinness

#7386. How a sports betting creator drove 200 million short-form views of his podcast

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of an ongoing series examining successful creator business strategies. More from the series →

After some trial and error, Zach “Mystic Zach” Hirsch has determined how to toe the content line. The sports betting analyst and commentator has nearly 2 million Instagram followers, and a Draft Kings-sponsored podcast, Double Coverage, that boasts millions of views across YouTube and TikTok.

But not long ago, one of Hirsch’s biggest videos (both in terms of views and in terms of guests, as it was an interview with fellow creator-turned-boxer Jake Paul) was taken off YouTube, reportedly because it was sponsored by an offshore betting company Hirsch wouldn’t name, rather than one of the more mainstream platforms like Draft Kings, which is vetted by Google and available state-by-state depending on local gambling laws. The offshore sportsbooks exist in a sort of legal gray area — they’re unlicensed in the U.S., but federal law targets the operators and payment processors rather than the bettors themselves. Still, the takedown threw his content approach into relief and forced him to take a step back.

“That was really scary,” said Hirsch. He said the company wasn’t an approved Google vendor, which seemingly prompted the strike, but didn’t provide details.

This spring, Hirsch found that partner in Draft Kings, which he calls an “anchor sponsor.” There’s exclusivity, but only in the category of sportsbooks and prediction markets, so he’s free to get other partnerships, and the increased social acceptance of legal betting brands has made it fairly easy for him to find new partners, including direct-to-consumer telehealth and online pharmacy brand Hims. He did not say how much his partnerships are worth — or how long they will last.

“Sports betting hasn’t been a notable flag for brands when they vet creators,” said Mat Micheli, co-CEO and co-founder of Viral Nation. “Betting is now deeply ingrained in the culture of the brands that deem it important.”

However, Micheli stressed the importance of partnering with licensed, regulated vendors to avoid what happened to Hirsch last year.

“There are regulated multinational operators in other parts of the world that are legitimate and licensed, just not in the United States. For American creators speaking to an American audience, the standard has to be simple: you only promote something that is lawful to use in your audience’s own country. That’s the line that separates a brand-safe partnership from a risky one,” he said.

Hirsch’s creator career is deeply entwined with the controversial world of sports betting. He signed a contract with BetOnline as soon as he turned 18, and spent nearly five years with them, co-hosting a sports prediction podcast with former NFL player Adam “Pacman” Jones. At the time, however, he was just a paid host, and not involved in the show’s day-to-day production or marketing efforts.

Hirsch decided he wanted to take a more hands-on approach for his next podcast endeavor.

“I saw a lot of soft spots and inconsistencies that could be improved, especially as a Gen Zer,” he said. The biggest soft spot for him was the lack of reliance on other social media platforms to drive discovery. “Eighty-five percent of Gen Z sports podcast listeners are introduced through social media, which is clips,” Hirsch said, referencing a 2024 Edison Research report.

He leveraged his existing connections in the professional sports and entertainment worlds, which he built with a different podcast, Kiss and Myst, co-hosted by rap legend Jadakiss (Hirsh said the podcast was sponsored by World Star Hiphop for seven figures). These connections helped ensure Double Coverage would have an impressive slate of guests, from WWE wrestler Cody Rhodes to boxing legend Mike Tyson, as well as a regular slate of NFL players and UFC fighters, and leaned hard into circulating clips on TikTok and YouTube.

“We’ve had over 200 million views on short-form content since the NFL season began,” Hirsh said. Since July, the podcast has seen steady, impressive growth, with 151 million cumulative views on TikTok, and 43.7 million on TikTok, per the platform’s statistics shared with Digiday.

“I’ve gone all in on short-form content and identifying what resonates most,” Hirsh said. That includes shaving off a half-second or word to make the clip more punchy and hiring a professional designer for thumbnails. In its second season, Hirsch pivoted to make the podcast more fast-paced so it can easily be cut up into clips.

“There’s no room to breathe; it’s like you’re watching one continuous TikTok clip, which is what resonates with our audience,” he said.

For Hirsh, short-form video is not just a promotional channel; it shapes how the show is made. He says the team edits clips tightly, invests in thumbnails, and has made the second season faster-paced so moments can be cut for social platforms.

Double Coverage is also looking beyond the screen, with plans for more experiential programming and in-person events. Hirsh said his ability to “set up anywhere, travel anywhere, fit into any crowd” could help make that possible. He doesn’t have an official NFL partnership, but he says he is, “in talks” and plans on having a “massive media day presence” for the second year in a row.

“I’d love to get 100 million views a week on short-form content and get over a million views a podcast episode,” he said. “I’d like to have four anchor sponsors that believe in me and are committed long term that I can build around.”

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

Yahoo is the first licensee of the Internet Licensing Collective, offering publishers a new way to get paid for content used in AI search.

The IAB’s new AI attribution framework promises to standardize measurement, but a lack of platform data leaves much of AI’s impact invisible.

#7384. TwitchCon is at a crossroads as creators question its value

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

TwitchCon remains a draw for brands and streaming companies, but some creators say the cost, safety concerns and uncertain payoff have made the annual convention harder to justify.

Ahead of TwitchCon 2026’s kick off this weekend in San Diego, allegations spread on X that Twitch was giving away tickets, and that ticket counts for Twitch Partners, the top-performing, monetized creators, were the lowest they’d been in years. Though Twitch wouldn’t outright say attendance was down, a spokesperson told Digiday that “events have changed a lot in the past few years,” after the post-COVID “crazy high demand.” They confirmed the company was giving away tickets through their Ambassador program, which it has done in previous years.

Digiday spoke with six creators and two brands showing up at the convention about what this might signal for the streaming platform’s biggest annual event and the state of the streaming creator economy.

“I don’t feel like we have a clear enough understanding of what TwitchCon is today and how I could add value to its programming by being there,” said Justin Miclat, chief growth officer at talent and brand management company Click Media. Miclat also founded the Kinetic Group talent agency, which has worked with live streamers. Last year, the group spent $50,000 producing a TwitchCon event promoting and showcasing women in the industry.

TwitchCon, the IRL event for the livestream platform Twitch, which accrued 900 million hours of watch time last year, has historically been a place where creators connect with fans, sponsors and their peers. Past and present sponsors include State Farm, e.l.f. Cosmetics, Streamlabs, Southwest Airlines, Honda, and the platform’s parent company, Amazon Prime. Creators often spend the weekend seeking out new partnerships opportunities.

Recently, however, TwitchCon (which is typically held in California every year along with a European counterpart) has faced scrutiny from attendees around security issues, rising costs, less opportunities and a lack of clarity around what the platform considers its core community. Top creator Emily-Beth “Emiru” Schunk was assaulted during a meet-and-greet at TwitchCon 2025, prompting backlash and calls for tighter security (and a statement from Twitch). Others pointed to a rash of IRL streamers’ bad behavior while livestreaming at or around the San Diego Convention Center.

Mike Murphy O’Reilly, chief revenue officer at Dexerto, a gaming publisher that is also a full-service creative and marketing agency, said TwitchCon is still a “core event” for the company. “It’s one of the few events across the year where you have a mix of both endemic and non-endemic clients,” he said. Still, he noted that this year’s event “does feel quieter.”

For companies like Streamlabs, which offers a streaming platform regularly used by Twitch creators, the convention is still a must-attend and a must-sponsor. The company sets up content creation stations in the convention center, and creates TwitchCon-themed overlays for livestreams.

“At TwitchCon the percentage of attendees that are actually streamers is generally around 50 percent,” said Ashray Urs, head of Streamlabs. “For us, making broadcast software, that’s a great place to be.” Urs said the convention’s value lies in having so many streamers in one place to get consumer feedback and build new partnerships, as well as meeting key existing brand partners like NVIDIA.

“It’s a perfect endemic fit for us,” Urs said.

But is TwitchCon the perfect fit for creators?

Streamer Tevin “YourBudTevin” Lolar, who has over 50,000 followers on Twitch, told Digiday that creators attending the convention usually fall into one of three categories: low-level streamers looking to learn more and make connections, mid-level streamers hoping to connect with brands, and top streamers, who host events and establish “big money partnerships and deliverables.”

TwitchCon began as an IRL event focused on leveraging its tentpole creators, But as major stars like Kai Cenat and Darren Jason “IShowSpeed” Watkins Jr. have moved beyond the need for the convention, Twitch may have to redefine how it serves creators and fans.

“Twitch needs to hone in on the community-driven streamers and bring them up in order to maintain the health of the convention,” Lolar said. The Twitch spokesperson said the platform wants TwitchCon to reflect the growing community of streamers, while ensuring “gaming is still the heart” of the event.

For mid-tier creators like Veronica “Nikatine” Ripley, a Twitch Ambassador (an initiative that recognizes community leaders on the platform) with over 30,000 followers, TwitchCon isn’t a viable expense anymore. She attended the convention from 2018 through 2023, and was even featured in 2022 marketing materials.

“Do I stand to make more money attending a convention through business deals or meeting potential product vendors or catching up with sponsors? Or do I make more money just streaming instead? In the case of TwitchCon, unfortunately the latter has been true for some time,” she said.

Amira “Xmiramira” Virgil, feels similarly. She was in the first class of Twitch Ambassadors and attended TwitchCon in 2018 and 2019, but the ROI isn’t worth it for her anymore. “The brands I want to work with can be found at conventions and events that don’t cost me upwards of $3,000, and even if they do, there’s still a better ROI,” she said. “I’ve also heard some critique about what brands are attending as well.”

Frogan, a streamer with nearly 70,000 followers who prefers to go by her social media handle, said the event is “way too expensive” and that there are very few incentives for partnered creators.

Quin “CtrlAltQuin” Martin, who has over 10,000 followers on the platform, said stressed the cost. “Traveling to the other side of the country to create free content for Twitch just doesn’t make sense to me at this moment – though I love the platform,” they said.

Nearly everyone Digiday spoke with raised safety concerns. Urs said past TwitchCons felt like there were cameras on you at all times, noting the high volume of IRL creators streaming on the convention floor (this year, Twitch introduced lanyards that signal attendees’ comfort level with being on camera). O’Reilly mentioned the Emiru incident as a detractor for potential attendees.

“Accessibility can sometimes be an afterthought for events inside and outside the ‘con,” said Martin, who uses a mobility device. “Pair that with minimal security and folks who don’t understand boundaries, it can be a hot mess.”

Frogan attended her first TwitchCon in 2022, and said she requested security for 2023’s event after allegedly receiving death threats for her vocal support of Palestine. In 2024, she and several other streamers were temporarily banned from the platform after a Twitch-approved panel held on-stage during TwitchCon drew online backlash and accusations of antisemitism, which she vehemently denies.

“I don’t feel safe going to the event from both physical interactions and Twitch as a company in general,” Frogan said. “I feel like they have failed me as a creator multiple times so it’s not worth it for my peace of mind.”

“We’ve taken the concerns raised following last year’s TwitchCon very seriously,” said the Twitch spokesperson, adding ahead of this weekend’s event, the company has increased investment in security, added more trained safety, and strengthened escalation procedures for on-site teams.

“Our goal is to create an environment where everyone attending TwitchCon feels safe, heard and excited to be there, and we’ll continue listening to our community and making improvements.”

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

Yahoo is the first licensee of the Internet Licensing Collective, offering publishers a new way to get paid for content used in AI search.

The IAB’s new AI attribution framework promises to standardize measurement, but a lack of platform data leaves much of AI’s impact invisible.

#7380. Why brands like Anthropologie are commissioning creator-created shows instead of posts

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Pooja Tripathi created Brooklyn Coffee Shop, the comedy web series built around a barista with an opinion about every customer who walks through the door. The show won a Webby this year and its fan club in marketing circles keeps growing.

SharkNinja paid to work its Auto Barista machine into an episode, Anthropologie went a step further and hired Tripathi to write and lead its first micro comedy series — a holiday party caper she took from script to shoot. Even instagram has noticed. Tessa Lyons, its vp of product singled out the show as one her favorites while talking up the app’s renewed push into longer-form content.

All of which has turned Tripathi into a social showrunner of sorts, operating in a corner of the creator economy where more brands are starting to shop.

Take Anthropologie – the brand’s marketers were already fans of Tripathi’s work before they got in touch earlier this year.

“We engaged her because we’re fans of her work,” said Barbra Sainsurin, global CEO at Anthropologie Group. The admiration turned out to be mutual and Tripathi “leaped at the chance to create a series for us,” she said. Her comic voice was the draw for the brand, which wanted to try its hand at comedy even though “humour and entertainment haven’t been voices or characters that we’ve incorporated in our story telling traditionally,” said Sainsurin.

The result was the brand’s first micro comedy series “The Holiday Party,” a twist on the seasonal misadventure trope that sits inside its “On My Way” seasonal campaign, which went live last month. Each episode is 90 seconds and will air on the brand’s social channels between November 11–19 alongside behind the scenes footage.

Tripathi developed the characters and the direction of the show while Anthropologie’s team took charge of the styling, from what each character wears to how the party itself was decorated. The goal was a setting that felt true to Tripathi’s audience and also looked like “a party that an Anthro person would throw” said Sainsurin.

The brand took a few more pages from the entertainment playbook for the rollout. Anthropologie is “treating it like a movie launch,” with trailers, teasers and hooks at the end of each episode to pull viewers into the next. Instagram and TikTok are getting the series as are YouTube, Pinterest and Reddit, which means it will be “distributed more broadly than it has ever,” said Sainsurin.

Whether it goes further depends on the numbers. Sainsurin’s team is watching earned reach, how many non-followers the series lands in front of and “how far is this content travelling in the algorithm”. Saves, shares, comments, site visits, product page views and sales will come into the picture once the full spread of holiday content has run. That’s a big shift for a team that doesn’t even do much with creators now. They make up a smaller share of the media mix than at other brands, said Sainsurin.

Putting a price on that kind of work is its own puzzle. Hyphen, the Reign Maker Group-owned talent management firm that brokers series deals, splits the bill into the creator’s fee, production, usage rights, exclusivity and the number of episodes. On its own roster, a single sponsored post from a mid-tier lifestyle creator starts at around $8,000 to $10,000. A multi episode series lands “well into five figures,” and paid usage plus category exclusivity can push the total to six, said Victoria Bachan, CEO of Hyphen. Her team tells brands to weigh that against “ what it would cost to build that audience habit themselves,” she continued.

More brands are starting to have that conversation. They’re hiring social show runners like Tripathi, sometimes in place of part of their influencer marketing spend and sometimes alongside it. In doing so they’ve reopened one of the industry’s longest-running arguments: whether influencer marketing and creator marketing are different things at all.

Both pay someone with an audience to put a product in front of it but they arguably split from there. Influencer marketing leans on the person, whose recommendation and follower count do the heavy lifting on a post that lives and dies on their own account. Creator marketing, on the other hand, heads off toward the content itself. Think characters that viewers get attached to and a format they come back to week after week. All of that sits with the show itself so it holds value well after the influencer post has scrolled out of view.

Take the show We Are MICE, a mockumentary set at a fictional live events agency whose name stands for meetings, incentives, conferences and exhibitions. Beauty creator Genevieve Turley, in her first acting role, plays Maggie, who manages the agency’s team. She’s a 40-something parent who is nearly divorced and recently back at work, trying to pull off flawless events with a team that’s some way short of flawless itself. The clients are real as in they’re the brands that sponsor the series and the plot follows Maggie’s team as it tries to deliver an event for them. Laura Geller Beauty is the first, commissioning six episodes between 60 and 90 seconds that started rolling out across Instagram and TikTok last month (October 26).

The format comes from Rooscreen, the Royal Television Society-nominated production company behind comedy short film Mourning Glory, and was developed with brand partnerships strategists DASH Talent. Like plenty of sitcoms before it, MICE started life in a writer’s room, built around an idea Rooscreen founder Becky Rooney had been carrying around for a while. The characters and their world came first, in other words, and the brands were invited in after.

“Yes, it’s an advert and yes it’s a commercial but rather than a typical product placement or doing a standard influencer post we’ve tried to get into the storyline with the brand,” said Rooney.

“I wanted it to be funny and edgy but also brand safe,” she added.

The plan is for each new series to bring in a different brand and a different event while Maggie and her colleagues, who are also creators, carry their storylines from one to the next. Which leaves room for a different member of the cast to step forward each time, depending on whose audience best fits the brand. A sportswear deal, for instance, would hand the spotlight to Austyn Farrell, a creator and keen marathon runner who plays Mo on the show. “These creators understand their audiences so who better to guide how we advertise certain brands than the people who actually really understand that product,” said Rooney.

Early numbers appear to back that up, or at least as far as views and interactions can tell. As of earlier this week, the episodes had collectively hit 1.3 million views on Instagram with 11,000 interactions.

“Going viral is one thing but if that means reaching two million people who don’t want to buy the product then does that have as much value as reaching 20,000 people who actually do?,” said Rooney.

None of which means brands are turning their backs on creator scale. Anthropologie, for instance, has around 50 creators working on the same campaign. Some are returning partners. Others came through an agency to reach audiences the brand isn’t getting to yet. Similarly SharkNinja works with Tripathi as well as Alex Berry, John Connor Hammon and Ari Caga — the three creators of The ick vertical digital web series — while continuing to work with large groups of creators.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

#7378. Product Owner - Innovation Team (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is the maker of Imunify360 — a suite of security products protecting millions of websites across the web hosting industry — and of CloudLinux OS, the operating system behind a large share of the world's shared hosting. To learn more about our company and products, visit our websites: cloudlinux.com and imunify360.com. Alongside those established product lines we run a small innovation team whose job is to find and prove the next ones.

We are looking for a Product Owner to lead that team: a small, senior, fully remote squad of engineers that takes new product concepts from idea to a working MVP in customers' hands, then hands proven products to a product line and moves on to the next concept. You will be accountable for the pipeline of ideas, the speed and rigour with which they are tested, and the evidence behind every go / no-go recommendation.

This is a hands-on lead role, not pure people management: roughly 50% discovery and leadership (customer conversations, scoping, prioritisation, coaching, reporting to the CPO) and 50% building (prototypes, spikes, design and code review, demo stands). You will not be the team's only builder, but you must be able to build a credible prototype yourself and hold a technical opinion about every part of it.

You don't need to arrive as an expert in web hosting or security. If you have taken a product from nothing to its first paying customers before — as a founder, an early engineer, or the lead of an incubation team — and you get deep into unfamiliar technical territory fast, we will teach you the market.

The innovation team sits across CloudLinux and Imunify and owns the product concepts that do not yet have a home in an established product line. Ideas come from customer conversations, industry events, telemetry across more than 80,000 servers and the CPO’s strategy; the team’s job is to turn them into something a customer will pay for within a quarter, using AI coding agents throughout.

We evaluate engineers partly on how effectively they work with coding agents, and in the innovation team this is the main lever on speed. As the lead you model and coach it: prompt specificity, review quality on agent-generated code, context provision, iteration and error recovery, and end-to-end task completion. Expect to use agents to build prototypes, generate test corpora and draft customer-facing material.

#7377. LinkedIn brings its B2B ad targeting to U.K. connected TVs

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

In its latest effort to capitalize on growing demand for video, LinkedIn is expanding its CTV ads to the U.K.

The launch follows its staged U.S. rollout, which began in April 2024, before becoming widely available across the U.S. and Canada in 2025. It brings LinkedIn’s professional audience targeting to CTV, allowing advertisers to reach U.K. decision-makers by job role, industry and seniority while they watch ad-supported streaming content. For example, a software company looking to reach senior IT decision-makers could use LinkedIn’s targeting to serve CTV ads to viewers in relevant job roles and industries, rather than relying solely on broader demographic or household-level targeting.

So far, the platform has partnered with LG and Titan OS for its U.K. CTV ads offering, and it’s available to all advertisers in the market.

“When you think about professional identities and how you want to reach and engage with them, we’re a platform where people tell us who they are,” said Jae Oh, senior director of product management at LinkedIn. “They get verified for the jobs that they have, and we’re able to support a higher fidelity of reach when it comes to how you reach those audiences at scale.”

LinkedIn is positioning CTV as more than just an awareness play, arguing that its professional audience targeting can help advertisers reach decision-makers earlier in the buying process and influence them further down the funnel.

“For so long CTV has been seen as like you naturally think it’s an awareness play, but actually where we’ve seen a real success is when clients use it as part of a full-funnel strategy,” said Milka Privodanova, vice president of LinkedIn Marketing Solutions, large customers — international at LinkedIn.

The company claims that people exposed to a brand’s CTV ad cost 30% less to convert into sales leads. Cisco, which has already tested the targeting update, saw 52% incremental reach beyond its LinkedIn in-feed activity, 141% higher lead form completion rates and 35% higher click-through rates among audiences exposed to its CTV ads, according to its LinkedIn case study.

But the proposition comes with questions over cost and targeting accuracy. Accuracast, for example, evaluated LinkedIn’s CTV offering when it was only available in the U.S. and found its pricing significantly higher than other direct CTV providers, alongside high minimum spend requirements.

“There’s definitely a business case for it — our B2B clients would love to get more reach and eyeballs on connected TVs — but if we’re going to pay LinkedIn’s usual premium pricing, we’d want more clarity on how LinkedIn plans to accurately target their users on these devices,” said the agency’s group CEO Farhad Divecha.

The idea is that advertisers can use CTV to introduce their brand to decision-makers, retarget and nurture those audiences through video and other LinkedIn ad formats to encourage them to take action. The platform is also looking to improve how advertisers measure CTV’s contribution to business outcomes beyond clicks and lead form completions. Oh said LinkedIn is also investing in revenue and pipeline attribution, alongside incrementality testing, to help advertisers understand CTV’s contribution to business outcomes.

“You will see deeper investment when it comes to incrementality testing or experimentation to prove that these different tactics you’re employing work for you,” Oh said.

The U.K. CTV expansion gives LinkedIn another way to tap into the demand for video, by  extending its video offering beyond the platform and onto the TV screen.

Video advertising on LinkedIn is growing 25% year-over-year, according to Privodanova, though she declined to share how much CTV contributes to that growth.

LinkedIn has been stitching together the pieces of its CTV business since 2024 when it lined up Roku and Samsung Ads as supply partners and launch LinkedIn Premier, a managed product built on NBCUniversal’s streaming inventory, A deal for premium Paramount inventory followed in mid-2025, along with integrations with Innovid and Sprinkly meant to make CTV campaigns easier to run.

“As a premium advertising platform, LinkedIn will need to demonstrate that its audience data can translate into meaningful reach and measurable impact,” said Andy Hunt, head of paid media at agency Brave Bison. “Targeting professionals through household devices presents its own challenges around signal quality, accuracy and attribution. Advertisers will want to understand how effectively CTV exposure can be connected to wider campaign performance, rather than simply adding another awareness metric.”

#7372. Media Briefing: The IAB finds AI measurement standards elusive for publishers amid platform data gaps

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Briefing covers the latest in media trends for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Thursday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Media Briefing looks at how the IAB’s months-long effort to standardize AI attribution ran into a roadblock: the lack of visibility into what happens inside AI platforms.

The Interactive Advertising Bureau spent months trying to determine how the industry should attribute and credit conversions influenced by AI. But it ran into a problem: you can’t standardize what you can’t see.

Digiday first reported in August that the IAB was developing an AI attribution framework to standardize measuring and crediting AI’s role in conversions, and the signals to quantify AI’s influence on purchase decisions. The IAB’s working group included tech companies, publishers, agencies, measurement vendors and brands — including People Inc., Walmart, WPP Media, Emberos, Criteo, Bluefish, Microsoft, Brandlight, Tinuiti and Prudential Financial. The resulting “AI-Era Attribution Blueprint,” was published today.

The blueprint addresses some of the questions behind the effort, but it also underscores how much remains unmeasurable. Recommendations, consumer decisions and even transactions may happen inside AI platforms, but that data isn’t yet accessible to advertisers or measurement providers.

“Going in, I thought that we were going to help figure out how credit was assigned through the process. I realized actually what this document was starting to become was more of a call to action to the industry,” Caroline Giegerich, the IAB’s vp of AI said during a virtual IAB roundtable on Monday. “There are evidence layers that are missing. We’ve started to have conversations on that, but there’s still room to go. But without remarking on what’s missing, what we want to see, and AI’s influence in the purchase funnel, I don’t think we’re going to get there. So it kind of started in one place, and over the course of four months, ended in another.”

As Digiday previously reported, the measurement gap has prompted a growing number of startups to pursue solutions.

The IAB’s blueprint offers a framework for understanding AI’s role in driving business outcomes, while acknowledging that data gaps make its influence difficult to reliably measure and attribute. It also underscores a broader challenge for the industry: as publishers expand their GEO offerings to brands, measurement remains a major obstacle.

But publishers are experimenting with ways to address parts of the measurement gap. Time is working with Mobian to measure the performance of the ads it serves to AI agents, for example, and Bustle Digital Group is working with Profound to measure its GEO campaign performance for brands. Those efforts offer visibility into specific activity, but they don’t yet solve the broader challenges of tying AI’s influence to sales.

AI search makes it more difficult for brands to measure how LLMs influence consumer purchases, because impressions, clicks and referral data aren’t always available when an AI assistant guides consumers from product discovery to purchase decisions. And even when an AI recommendation does lead to a sale, those conversions can be incorrectly attributed to search, direct traffic, or other channels, according to the IAB’s report.

That attribution gap can distort marketing investment decisions, with spend going to channels that capture conversions rather than those that influenced them. Similarweb data cited in IAB’s report found consumers who received a brand recommendation from an AI assistant are 2.5 times more likely to visit that brand’s website within seven days, but only 8.8% of those visits come directly from AI referrals. Instead, 55.9% are attributed to search and 19.9% to direct traffic.

The IAB framework focuses on three areas. It classifies who or what created the intent, made the decision, and executed the transaction. It defines additional claims AI involvement creates, and the evidence each one requires. And it highlights the transparency and measurement gaps that make those claims difficult to verify. (More detail is available in the framework).

The clearest case of this gap is when publisher content is behind an AI recommendation, the IAB noted in its report. If a consumer asks an AI answer engine for the best option in a category, and the answer draws on a publisher’s review, the consumer may not click the citation (a link to the publisher’s review), and it gets reported as a search or direct sale by the retailer.

“The review shaped the sale, and the publisher is eligible for a contributory claim. The evidence differs depending on how publisher content informed the answer. Content used in model training is generally not traceable from outside the model. Grounding and citation are more observable: publishers may be able to see when their content is cited or reflected in an AI response, although citation alone does not establish how much that content influenced the answer. What remains largely missing is the connection between those sourcing signals and downstream conversions. Until that link exists, the contributory claim cannot be fully substantiated,” the IAB report reads.

The IAB’s recommendations span AI platforms, brands, agencies and publishers. Platforms should disclose whether recommendations are paid or organic, which sources inform them and label AI-originated traffic consistently.

Brands and agencies should apply the framework’s claim-and-evidence standards across platforms, while publishers should identify claims about their content’s influence and the evidence needed to support them, per the report.

“Publishers are feeding the answers but they don’t have the receipts,” said Jaime Schultheis, head of global data partnerships at Bombora, where she leads a network of B2B publishers, brand sites and data providers.

The IAB framework does accomplish defining the parts of the AI measurement journey, which can help publishers compare these between vendors and their different measurement structures, Jon Roberts, People Inc.’s chief innovation officer, said during the roundtable.

“Everyone’s pitching you on their custom proprietary measurement solution, and they’re all using the same terms, and the terms all mean something different because they’ve all designed these in a silo and are racing to get your business, and they’re all unique,” he said.

There are some signs that the measurement gap may narrow soon. The U.K.’s Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) is requiring Google to provide U.K. publishers with clickthrough data for its AI search features by Dec. 3. OpenAI is also testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers and how many clicks those appearances send to its site.

“We’re waiting [with] bated breath,” said Graham Howes, svp of distribution and AI intelligence at BDG, adding that this data from OpenAI and Google would be incredibly valuable for its GEO offering.

During the roundtable, Roberts noted that without this data, publishers are effectively “flying blind.” Sharing analytics benefits both publishers and AI platforms by helping them identify business opportunities, improve products and better serve their users, he said. Third-party measurement would still be important for independently verifying platforms’ own data, Roberts added.

Brands want this measurement gap solved just as much as publishers. Ross Bucholc, associate media director at Prudential, said in the roundtable that the lack of first-party data from AI platforms is holding back his ability to unlock more ad budgets going to LLMs.

Even with better visibility into AI activity, connecting that activity to sales remains difficult. Josh Blyska, head of insights at Profound, told Digiday that existing attribution tools struggle to connect AI search’s influence on consumer purchase decisions to conversions. Platforms like Profound can measure AI visibility, citations and how brands appear in AI answers, but can’t reliably track whether those interactions lead to purchases.

In a recent analysis of browser-session data, Profound found one in three consumers had a relevant AI conversation about their purchase within a month that went unattributed, Blyska said. (The dataset didn’t include Google’s AI Mode or AI Overviews.)

“It’s a really tricky problem right now,” he said.

Ultimately, the challenge is not just measuring a brand’s visibility within AI platforms but proving that visibility drives business results. John Sills, head of sales, North America at The Guardian, said the industry needs a more consistent way to understand how AI patterns surface and answer questions.

“We’re not measuring CTRs here. There’s no way of really understanding where the attribution is coming from,” he said. “These LLMs are in the ad business now… In the same way you can call your account manager at Facebook and try to optimize a social campaign or on YouTube, we need that level of account management to help guide the best practices.”

“We are capable, and AI companies are capable of reaching deals that give us fair compensation for content and referring to our site. Ninety-five percent of the AI referrals we get now come through OpenAI. So to me, that’s a win for them and a win for us if that’s how people want to access the news. So when people say deals like that aren’t possible or we don’t deserve compensation, I don’t really buy it.”

— Matt Murray, Washington Post executive editor, speaking about its deal with OpenAI, at Web Summit in Lisbon, Portugal.

$330 million: The reported price under consideration for The New York Times Company’s potential acquisition of Letterboxd, an online community for film enthusiasts.

2.7 billion: The number of times Ziff Davis’ content was cited in Google’s AI Overviews and AI Mode in Q3, nearly 10 times the clicks from Google Search those sites received.

37%: Ziff Davis’ YOY decline in Google referrals.

44%: BuzzFeed’s total YOY revenue decline in Q3 2026.

#7370. Yahoo becomes first licensee of ‘Spotify-like’ collective AI content licensing model

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

AI content licensing has largely comprised of one-off deals between publishers and tech companies. RSL, which is being renamed The Internet Licensing Collective (ILC), is pursuing a collective model instead, with Yahoo as its first licensee.

The deal means publishers of all sizes can license their content for use in Yahoo’s AI-powered search engine Scout and get paid based on how that content is used.

Digiday spoke with ILC co-founder Doug Leeds about how this model works and how publishers can earn revenue.

A licensee like Yahoo pays a blanket licensing fee for access to content in the collective. Leeds declined to say how much Yahoo was paying, but the fee is tied to the “demand-side revenue.”  Yahoo declined to be interviewed for this story.

As a licensee’s business scales, the amount available for publisher compensation can grow as well.

“It allows businesses that are making a lot of money off of the content to keep that content in place and sustainable. But it also allows for entrants,” Leeds said. “As the revenue scales, the percentage scales. So more money comes out. But that also means that entrants can come in, and there can be new use cases and new players and new products, and all of that’s important to the evolution of what we’re doing to the market.”

A new report out today from publishing analytics firm Mather Economics estimated that collective licensing for AI could generate publisher compensation equal to 15% to 35% of AI company revenue, a potential market that in three years could surpass the U.S. display advertising market. The analysis was based on the current annual revenue from the two biggest frontier models, OpenAI and Anthropic, and an economic model created by Mather Economics incorporating AI training-data scaling laws.

Leeds likened the licensing collective to a Spotify-like model.

“It’s kind of like Spotify, but also a song is a song is a song, and content is very different. And so we had to come up with these rules that made sense specifically for digital content,” Leeds said.

Leeds said ILC worked closely with its publisher collective to figure out those rules. The first step was to define a “segmented royalty pool.” This separates licensing revenue by AI company, rather than combining payments into a single fund shared by all participating publishers. That means publishers only receive a share of the revenue generated by AI products that actually use their content.

More AI and tech companies are testing pay-per-value licensing models. Digiday has previously reported that Google and Microsoft are testing and developing these models, which compensate publishers when their content meaningfully contributes to AI-generated responses, rather than paying based on how frequently their content is used. It’s a shift from flat-fee licensing deals to more dynamic pricing, based on the value publishers’ content delivers to AI companies.

When an AI company signs a licensing agreement with ILC, that money goes into its own royalty pool. Rather than combining all these payments into one enormous fund and distributing them across participating publishers, ILC keeps the royalty pools separate.

This protects specialized publishers from having their revenue diluted, Leeds explained. A general-purpose AI search engine like Yahoo Scout might use large quantities of content from hundreds or thousands of publishers, but a specialized healthcare AI product might use a much smaller amount of content from a handful of medical-focused publishers, and that specialized content could be considerably more valuable to the healthcare company, he said.

ILC wanted to ensure that a publisher’s compensation reflects the value of the AI product using its content, not simply how much content it contributes to the overall licensing ecosystem.

“An AI vertical in health might be very low volume, but much higher specific value. So if your content gets used in that, you should split that high value, not get diluted by all the content that got used by Yahoo,” Leeds said.

Over 3,000 publishers are now part of the ILC, he noted.

The payments are determined by what Leeds called “allocation rules.” These take into account how much content is used, like grounding, retrieval or displayed in an AI response, and whether the publisher is credited or cited as a source.

For example, an AI product that uses a publisher’s content to check a fact might receive a lower allocation than one that displays detailed election results and explicitly credits the Associated Press, Leeds said. He did not disclose the exact formula used to calculate each publisher’s share.

The allocation rules are intended to discourage AI companies from choosing content simply because it’s cheaper, and instead incentivize them to use what they consider the highest-quality sources, he said.

Publishers can also decide who they want to license to. Right now, their only option is Yahoo. But as more licensees sign up, they can go into their ILC portal and select which AI companies they do and don’t want to license their content to. For example, if a publisher signs a separate content licensing deal with an AI company, they can take their content out of the collective. In the portal, they can also see what their content is being used for, such as RAG or training.

RSL, which stood for the Really Simple Licensing (RSL), was two things: a group of publishers working on a collective licensing agreement, and the organization behind the RSL Protocol, a standard that publishers can use in robots.txt to describe in machine-readable language what their terms of service are. Publishers can ask AI companies for a pay-per-crawl or pay-per-inference fee through the RSL Protocol, for example. “Thousands” are currently using this standard, Leeds said.

But Leeds wanted a new name for the non-profit organization that reflected the publisher collective, to make it clear that this is a licensing model that any publisher can join, he said.

Leeds said ILC is currently in talks with “some” of the major AI companies interested in the licensing collective. He declined to name which ones.

“You can do a deal with a one-off, and that’s fantastic. But that doesn’t invest in the infrastructure of content in general,” he said.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

The IAB’s new AI attribution framework promises to standardize measurement, but a lack of platform data leaves much of AI’s impact invisible.

Ahead of TwitchCon 2026, brands and creators weigh in on if the platform’s big IRL event is worth the cost and potential safety issues.

#7366. Why user-first ad experiences build the strongest app businesses

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Today’s mobile users are searching, streaming, scrolling and shopping in bursts as they go about their day. According to RealityMine, the average smartphone user interacts with around 18 different apps per day, racking up to 200 app sessions daily.¹ In each of those moments, attention is won second by second — and lost just as quickly.

Capturing value in those seconds is remarkably difficult. When audience acquisition is expensive, and engagement is just as hard to earn, app studios cannot afford ad experiences that cause churn.

In-app ads are a core part of the overall app experience, yet monetization and user retention are still too often treated as competing priorities. The assumption is that earning more revenue requires heavier ad loads, which damages the user experience, while protecting that experience means leaving money on the table.

“Disruptive ads have a severe, compounding negative impact on both current engagement and future user acquisition,” said Scott Sheffer, vp of global partnerships on Google’s sell-side monetization team. “Disruptive ads not only drive current users away but also prompt negative reviews, discouraging future users from downloading the app. Seems intuitive, but now there is data to back that.”

According to a Deloitte and Google AdMob study, a single encounter with a disruptive ad increases a user’s likelihood to churn by 6% to 7%.² When those experiences repeat, 52% of gamers say they leave the app for good.³ On the flip side, high-quality, user-first ad experiences actively protect engagement: 72% of gamers keep playing when ads are high quality⁴ and 20% report playing more often.⁵ These metrics demonstrate how ad quality serves as a fundamental growth engine beyond a cosmetic choice.

“App economics are not an either-or,” Sheffer said. “With quality ad experiences, users can stay engaged longer and you can naturally unlock more opportunities for other revenue streams, like in-app purchases and subscriptions.”

Sustainable revenue growth doesn’t come from aggressive ad strategies that disrupt audiences. It comes from pairing global, premium demand with uncompromising quality controls. This foundation protects in-app experiences and grows long-term revenue opportunities.

To truly scale revenue, safety cannot be restricted to a single network. Google built its advanced ad quality protections to extend across the AdMob network and participating third-party bidders. This means studios can filter out unwanted categories, block problematic creatives and apply brand rules across their bidding partners from a single interface.

Toss, a financial app company from South Korea’s Viva Republica, chose AdMob as its mediation partner to grow revenue through quality ad experiences. By using AdMob’s real-time ad review and rigorous A/B testing capabilities, Toss grew ad revenue 891% year over year while driving a 17% uplift in next-day retention for one of its app’s features.⁶

Across the wider app ecosystem, SensorTower data shows that apps primarily using AdMob’s mediation platform have a 25% higher 60-day and 90-day user retention rate than apps primarily using competing platforms.⁷˒⁸

“Our focus on quality and long-term growth explains why, in recent research, Kantar found that app developers rank Google AdMob No. 1 in both providing high-quality ads and in maximizing revenue — ahead of other leading app monetization platforms,” said Sheffer.⁹˒¹⁰

As app development cycles continue to accelerate, monetization operations must keep pace.

Instead of losing critical hours to manual creative review, app developer teams can lean on Google’s intelligent brand safety systems to help. For example, improved AI models in the Ad Review Center can swiftly flag off-brand creatives for removal. In parallel, click-through-rate-based filtering helps to block deceptive ad behaviors like forced redirects, fake buttons or hidden close icons. This tactic helps developers eliminate these formats across all bidding sources before they trigger player frustration.

For ads that need a closer look, revamped search filters in the Ad Review Center help teams find creatives faster — such as sorting creatives by revenue to prioritize the reviews that impact the bottom line.

By protecting both developer velocity and creative safety, teams can more easily grow revenue and focus on the user experience. That balance of automation and control is why a Kantar analysis showed app developers rate Google AdMob 1.5x higher than the competitor average for making it easy to monetize apps.¹¹

Delivering on ad quality and making monetization simple is only the start. Google is building new tools to help teams catch performance anomalies, implement and validate ad placements and preserve player retention.

“We believe enterprise-grade AI and monetization requires secure, native integration across the tools you already use today,” Sheffer said. “That is why Google AdMob is continuously expanding what an advanced monetization platform can do. By shifting routine diagnostics, complex yield optimizations and brand safety into intelligent, automated workflows, we are bridging the gap between app development and ad operations — so you can build, test and ship ad setups with the same speed and safety as your app code.”

Partner insights from Google Publisher Connections

Publisher Connections brings together publishers, buyers and their audiences. Across a suite of trusted platforms — from Google Ad Manager, AdMob and AdSense, to their buyer solutions — they deliver continuous innovation and powerful results.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

Local sports rights have gotten more complex and confusing as the regional sports network business changed. The leagues are now looking to simplify that offering.

#7362. Future of Marketing Briefing: Inside Guinness’ go-slow approach to creators

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of Marketing Briefing covers the latest in marketing for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Friday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

For a brand that takes nearly two minutes to pour, Guinness is in no great hurry where creators are concerned.

It has little interest in signing up creators en masse the way some of its rivals have. Rather, that side of its marketing will build organically, at the brand’s own pace, said global marketing manager and head of sport Paddy Carberry.

Speaking during a live recording of the Unofficial Partner sports business podcast in partnership with agency Sid Lee, the marketer made the case for why: “The minute we start getting involved in trends and social media stuff, all of a sudden we become the dad dancing at the disco.”

Few things kill a brand’s cool faster than chasing it — and Guinness has plenty to lose these days. Carberry traces much of the turnaround to the pandemic. With the pubs shut and most suppliers chasing unpaid bills, Guinness “extended our credit to all of our customers,” he continued, and it sent its unsold beer off to fertilize Christmas trees. Landlords and bar staff noticed, and that counted for a lot, since in Carberry’s view nobody has more sway over what gets ordered than the person pulling the pint. The brand’s ads played up the wait for the pub to come back so by the time the doors reopened, Guinness was one of the pints people had missed.

Guinness’ apprehension toward creators, therefore, is understandable. Its cool was earned behind the bar and a flood of sponsored posts could undo that work faster than a badly poured pint. Creators still played their part in the revival, mind. Most of them, however, weren’t on the payroll. London’s pint-rating accounts turned judging other people’s pours into a spectator sport and Kim Kardashian and Olivia Rodrigo did the brand plenty of favors without a Diageo brief. “Splitting the G” had been doing the rounds in pubs since at least 2018 when it earned an Urban Dictionary entry.

Over that period Guinness has nudged things along where it made sense. Its social team spent 2014 amplifying rituals drinkers had already invented and adding a layer of curated influencer partnerships. As Carberry tells it, the skill lies in knowing which moments to touch and which to leave well alone.

“While we lean into creators in appropriate ways we probably do less than you’d expect,” said Carberry.

And yet Carberry and his team seem more connected with how people view the brand than ever. Its “Singing Pints” campaign, which launched in 2023 but was revived over the summer, was inspired by the viral #SingingGuinness TikTok Trend where pubgoers poked faces into the creamy foam head of their pints and blew across them to make the foam “mouths” open and close.

“We took what was the consumer playing with the brand and amplified it into a TV spot,” said Carberry. “That was one of most successful pieces of content for the last 10 years.”

Creators are clearly part of Guinness’ future, just in a smaller role than the creator economy narrative would have it. Guinness seems more focused on community management and how creators fold into that. That puts it closer to where influencer marketing began. Before follower counts became a media currency, brands courted bloggers with tight-knit readerships and locals whose word carried on their own patch. The industry then spent a decade turning those relationships into a reach game. Guinness still takes part but it does so carefully.

“Guinness might not consciously be signing people up as creators, but ‘splitting the G’ has turned plenty of ordinary drinkers into exactly that,” said Jon Williams, founder and chief creative officer at Freedom Works. “They’re creating something to show their mates, in a pub, around a brand. That should be an ambition for any marketer: giving people something they want to do in real life, which then gives them something worth sharing on a screen.”

It also has less room for error than most brands. In the U.K., anyone drinking in an alcohol ad has to be, and look, over 25. The brand also can’t advertise anywhere where more than a quarter of the audience is under 18 and that’s a hard thing to promise on TikTok. The Advertising Standards Authority banned Au Vodka ads last year for exactly that, and Cranberry noted that advertising regulations are “stricter and stricter around the world.”

“While these results are above what we expected for this quarter, this level of growth is, of course, well below the level of growth that we expect from ourselves in the future… I’m encouraged by our progress, and I am proud of our team. But I want to be very clear, we are not satisfied.”

— The Trade Desk’s CEO Jeff Green when discussing the company’s quarterly revenues during the Q3 2026 earnings call.

34%: Average percentage of a brand’s video that TikTok viewers watch on the app

$3.4 million: Amount by which The Trade Desk’s revenue declined during Q3 2026

$34.1 million: Total CTV revenue that Nexxen reported during its Q3 earnings

14%: Percentage by which Nvidia’s stock dropped following the resignation of its CEO

OpenAI now lets brands publish a free “business agent” in ChatGPT, the organic twin of the sponsored agent it’s been selling as an ad since September.

Why brands like Anthropologie are commissioning creator-created shows instead of posts

Brands like Anthropologie are paying creators to make original series, such as short comedy shows, rather than one-off sponsored posts, betting it builds lasting audience habits, sometimes with money moved out of traditional influencer budgets.

LinkedIn brings its B2B ad targeting to U.K. connected TVs

LinkedIn is bringing its CTV ads to the U.K., letting advertisers target streaming viewers by job role, industry seniority, though agencies doubt the premium pricing and targeting accuracy are worth it.

Reddit is offering Q4 ad credits to win advertisers, but doubts about its measurement and ad tools make it hard to turn seasonal spend into year-round budgets, and slowing ad revenue growth and falling search referral traffic don’t help.

EU says ‘rabbit hole’ video feeds on TikTok violate its digital rules

The EU says TikTok’s For You feed breaks its digital rules by pulling users into harmful content “rabbit holes”, and it’s demanding redesigns, with fines up to 6% of revenue possible, per AP News.

A Morning Consult survey of about 2,000 Americans found only around a quarter of U.S. adults think creators reliably disclose paid brand deals, which points to a sizeable trust gap, per Tube Filter.

ByteDance is reportedly weeks away from launching a Muse-style AI assistant, according to The Next Web. Co-founder Zhang Yiming has reportedly told staff it may take until 2029 or 2030 to compete with Anthropic and OpenAI at the frontier.

#7359. AI is turning ad tech into a fight for the brain behind the buy

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The number of mergers and acquisitions in ad tech has been steadily increasing as 2026 draws to a close, proving an apt context for this year’s Digital Marketing Summit, hosted by investment bank LUMA Partners earlier this week, where attendees debated what’s most likely to spur the interest of corporate development teams

Opening the DMS proceedings were Terry Kawaja, LUMA Partners CEO, and Conor McKenna, a partner at the boutique investment bank, both of whom said AI is changing ad tech on two fronts: how marketing technology selects and delivers ads, and how consumers discover and buy products.

The first shift is in ad tech, with such technologies making previously complex workflows easier to automate; this movement is likely to prompt a shift in competitive advantage away from managing the process of campaign delivery to making better real-time decisions, i.e., firms that can better establish which signals to use, what creative to show, where to place an ad and how to improve results will thrive in the AI-first era.

“The workflow moat begins to evaporate with the AI technology that is coming out,” McKenna told attendees at the November 9 event, hosted in New York City.

This is likely to further intensify competition among demand-side platforms, supply-side platforms, agencies, retail media networks and major platforms over who controls advertising intelligence. Kawaja argued that efficiency matters, but effectiveness could matter much more. “I like to think of efficiency as the 10% incremental improvement, whereas the effectiveness could make a 10x difference,” he added.

For the open web to maintain its (modest) share of advertisers’ budgets — which the LUMA team described as fragmented compared with the scale and simplicity of large platforms — the challenge will be to deliver both easier workflows and demonstrably stronger outcomes.

The second shift is on the consumer side. AI assistants could bring research, discovery and comparison into a single interface, compressing the traditional purchase journey. The speakers expect people to remain involved for now, using agents to delegate tasks rather than handing over decisions entirely. If advertising becomes part of these experiences, consumer trust will be essential.

Together, these changes could reshape how advertisers attribute value, when it comes to the efficacy of their media spend, with several presenters at the event forecasting that such technologies will result in a more competitive market, as well as potential consolidation – a likely conclusion for investment bankers.

“Decisioning coupled with unique signal is what will differentiate the companies, separate the winners from the losers, and finally promote consolidation,” added Kawaja.

The key to success in such a scenario will be: scale, access to useful signals, decision-making intelligence and trust. However, the utmost metric will depend on whether platforms can show that their decisions improve results without undermining consumers’ confidence.

Speaking separately with Digiday on the sidelines of the event, Ana Milicevic, cofounder of Sparrow Advisers, described such technologies as: “Like this is the brain that we’re trying to outsource to a bunch of agents.”

Meanwhile, speaking separately with Digiday, Ravi Patel, co-founder and CEO of SWYM.ai, drew a distinction between setting budgets across platforms and picking individual impressions. Google, Meta and Amazon still make those impression-level decisions within their own systems, he said, describing unified media platforms, or UMPs, to quote the fast-emerging industry parlance. “It can manage the ‘what’ and the ‘how much.’ It can’t manage the ‘which impression.’ That distinction is the whole story,” he noted.

Local sports rights have gotten more complex and confusing as the regional sports network business changed. The leagues are now looking to simplify that offering.

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.
